Finally, the conditional dynamics of
\hat{\rho}^{c}
under heterodyne monitoring are governed by the stochastic measurement superoperator
\mathcal{S}[\sqrt{\gamma_{\rm H}}\hat{b}_{k}]
, given by:

\displaystyle\mathcal{S}[\sqrt{\gamma_{\rm H}}\hat{b}_{k}]\hat{\rho}^{c}=\sqrt{\frac{\gamma_{\rm H}}{2}}\!\left(\hat{b}_{k}\hat{\rho}^{c}+\hat{\rho}^{c}\hat{b}_{k}^{\dagger}-\langle\hat{b}_{k}+\hat{b}_{k}^{\dagger}\rangle^{c}\right)\!dW_{\mathcal{I}_{k}}\!(t)\penalty\

\displaystyle\penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ +\sqrt{\frac{\gamma_{\rm H}}{2}}\!\left(-i\hat{b}_{k}\hat{\rho}^{c}+i\hat{\rho}^{c}\hat{b}_{k}^{\dagger}-\langle-i\hat{b}_{k}+i\hat{b}_{k}^{\dagger}\rangle^{c}\right)\!dW_{\mathcal{Q}_{k}}\!(t)

\displaystyle\penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ +\gamma_{\rm H}\mathcal{D}[\hat{b}_{k}]\hat{\rho},

(23)

where
\langle\hat{o}\rangle^{c}
indicates the conditional expectation value of an arbitrary operator
\hat{o}
with respect to the measurement-conditioned quantum state,
\langle\hat{o}\rangle^{c}={\rm tr}\{\hat{\rho}^{c}\hat{o}\}
. Here
dW_{\mathcal{I}_{k}},dW_{\mathcal{Q}_{k}}
are independent Wiener increments describing measurement noise, satisfying
\langle dW_{\mathcal{I}_{k}}\rangle=\langle dW_{\mathcal{Q}_{k}}\rangle=0
, and
\langle dW_{\mathcal{I}_{k}}dW_{\mathcal{Q}_{k^{\prime}}}\rangle=0,\langle dW_{\mathcal{I}_{k}}dW_{\mathcal{I}_{k^{\prime}}}\rangle=\langle dW_{\mathcal{Q}_{k}}dW_{\mathcal{Q}_{k^{\prime}}}\rangle=\delta_{k,k^{\prime}}dt
. The Wiener increments are therefore related to the white noise terms introduced in Eq. (
4
) via
\xi_{\mathcal{I}_{k}}\equiv\frac{dW_{\mathcal{I}_{k}}}{dt},\xi_{\mathcal{Q}_{k}}\equiv\frac{dW_{\mathcal{Q}_{k}}}{dt}
. The classical readout noise terms in Eq. (
4
) are also taken to obey white noise statistics.

On the other hand, the quantum noise contributions
\xi^{\rm qm}_{\mathcal{I}_{k}},\xi^{\rm qm}_{\mathcal{Q}_{k}}
introduced in Eq. (
4
) generally do
not
obey white noise statistics. Quantum trajectories
\hat{\rho}^{c}
are conditioned on the heterodyne measurement record via the stochastic measurement superoperator
\mathcal{S}
. Quantum noise contributions depend on these quantum trajectories; more concretely, for example,
\xi_{\mathcal{I}_{k}}^{\rm qm}\!(t)=\langle\hat{X}_{k}\rangle^{c}-\langle\hat{X}_{k}\rangle
, and thus describes the stochastic deviation of an observable conditioned on a specific quantum trajectory from the ensemble average. Hence the quantum noise contributions inherit nontrivial correlation and noise properties from quantum trajectories. Lastly, monitoring of QNP modes opens them up to linear damping at the rate
\gamma_{\rm H}
, described by the standard dissipative superoperator
\mathcal{D}[\hat{o}]=\hat{o}\hat{\rho}\hat{o}^{\dagger}-\frac{1}{2}\{\hat{o}^{\dagger}\hat{o},\hat{\rho}\}
.

Definitions of all parameters characterizing the general measurement chain are summarized in Table
2
. Specific parameter values used to generate the various figures in the main text are summarized in Table
4
in the SI.

Appendix B
Truncated cumulants approach

For an arbitrary
\hat{\rho}
describing a quantum measurement chain with
R=M+K
modes, we can define its associated
characteristic function

\chi(\vec{w},\vec{w}^{*})

Carmichael 2002
,

\displaystyle\chi(\vec{w},\vec{w}^{*})=\mathrm{tr}\left\{\!\hat{\rho}\prod_{j=1}^{R}e^{iw_{j}^{\ast}\hat{o}_{j}^{\dagger}}\!\prod_{l=1}^{R}e^{iw_{l}\hat{o}_{l}}\!\right\}\!

(24)

where
\hat{o}_{j}\in\{\hat{a}_{m},\hat{b}_{k}\}
describes any mode of the complete quantum measurement chain, and
\vec{w}=(w_{1},\ldots,w_{R})
are auxiliary variables. Then, normal-ordered cumulants of the density matrix are formally defined via the log of the characteristic function (sometimes called the
generating function
),

\displaystyle C_{o_{1}^{\dagger p_{1}}\cdots o_{R}^{\dagger p_{R}}o_{1}^{q_{1}}\cdots o_{R}^{q_{R}}}\equiv\left.\frac{\partial^{n_{\rm ord}}\ln\chi(\vec{w},\vec{w}^{*})}{\prod_{j}\!\partial(iw_{j}^{\ast})^{p_{j}}\!\prod_{l}\!\partial(iw_{l})^{q_{l}}}\right|_{\vec{w}=\vec{w}^{\ast}=0}

(25)

where
n_{\rm ord}=p_{1}+\ldots+p_{J}+q_{1}+\ldots+q_{J}
defines the order of cumulants. Notwithstanding the complex formal definition, cumulants can be transparently related to more familiar operator expectation values: for a general operator
\hat{o}_{j}
, first-order cumulants
C_{o_{j}}\equiv\langle\hat{o}_{j}\rangle
are simply expectation values, while second-order cumulants
C_{o_{j}o_{l}}=\langle\hat{o}_{j}\hat{o}_{l}\rangle-\langle\hat{o}_{j}\rangle\langle\hat{o}_{l}\rangle
are their covariances. Expressions for higher-order cumulants become increasingly more involved, but can be systematically obtained, as discussed in the SI
SI
.

B.1
Numerical scheme: Stochastic Truncated Equations of Motion

The crucial advantage of cumulants as descriptors of a quantum state is that
specific
multimode quantum states admit particularly efficient representations when expressed in terms of cumulants. In particular, a quantum system in a product of coherent states is described entirely by its nonzero first-order cumulants; all cumulants with
n_{\rm ord}>1
vanish (see SI
SI
for derivations). Multimode quantum states that are defined entirely by their first
and
second-order cumulants admit Gaussian phase-space representations, and are thus labelled Gaussian states. States with nonzero cumulants of third or higher-order are thus by definition non-Gaussian states
Boutin et al. 2017
;
Ra et al. 2020
.

Our numerical approach leverages this economy of representation by using normal-ordered cumulants as a set of dynamical variables for quantum modes of the measurement chain. For example, for specific systems such as coherently-driven linear bosonic systems initialized in a Gaussian state, cumulants of order
n_{\rm ord}\leq 2
are sufficient, as such systems can be shown to persist in Gaussian states. If the QNPs considered in this paper were also linear, our model would satisfy this requirement. However, our interest is precisely in the role of the QNP nonlinearity. In this case, the cumulants describing the measurement chain and their dependencies are shown schematically in Fig.
8
for
M=K=1
. In particular, the nonlinearity generates states with nonzero cumulants of
n_{\rm ord}>2
. In the most general case, the dynamical equations for these cumulants couple to all orders, forming an infinite hierarchy of equations that does not close.

To obtain a tractable numerical method, we consider an ansatz wherein the quantum state of the complete measurement is described entirely by cumulants up to a finite order
n_{\rm ord}\leq n_{\rm trunc}
; all cumulants of order
n_{\rm ord}>n_{\rm trunc}
are thus set to zero, truncating the hierarchy and yielding a closed set of equations for the retained nonzero cumulants. In this paper, we choose
n_{\rm trunc}=2
for a quantum measurement chain defined entirely by its first and second-order cumulants, although the truncation can similarly be carried out at higher order. The resulting Stochastic Truncated Equations Of Motion (STEOMs) form the basis of our numerical approach in this paper.

We emphasize that the truncated cumulants ansatz has some important differences when compared to standard linearization approaches. As seen in Fig.
8
, note that both the nonlinearity and measurement terms couple second-order cumulants to first-order cumulants: this fact proves critical for the quantum state classification tasks we consider in this paper.

The utility of the STEOMs is naturally determined by the validity of the truncated cumulants ansatz. Since we are specifically interested in nonlinear quantum systems, which can generate higher-order cumulants in dynamics, one must ask when such an ansatz may hold. In the SI
SI
, we carry out a detailed analysis of the regimes of validity of this framework, together with benchmarking against exact solutions and full SME integration. We find that the STEOMs provide a very good approximation of full SME integration provided the strength of nonlinearity of QNP modes is weak relative to their loss rates; good agreement remains even up to
\Lambda_{k}/\gamma\sim 0.1
, which is around an order of magnitude larger than the strongest nonlinearity we consider in this paper.

Figure 8:
Schematic representation of stochastic truncated equations of motion (STEOMs), shown for simplicity for the
M=K=1
case. Black, red, purple, and blue lines indicate dependencies between cumulants due to measurement, QNP nonlinearity, system-QNP coupling, and system dynamics respectively. Dashed lines indicate dependencies on cumulants of order higher than
n_{\rm trunc}=2
in this case, which can arise due to the nonlinearity generating non-Gaussian states.

B.2
Semi-analytic scheme: Nonlinear van Kampen expansion based of the Fokker-Planck equation

In addition to enabling the (S)TEOMs as a practical numerical method for multimode measurement chains, the truncated cumulants approach is also central to our main analytic tool: a description of quantum dynamics that is perturbative in the nonlinearity of the measurement chain. This analysis is enabled by the close connection between normal-ordered cumulants and the positive-
{P}
representation via the characteristic function. More precisely, the positive-
{P}
representation is simply the Fourier transform of the characteristic function
Carmichael 2002
,

\displaystyle\mathcal{P}(\vec{\mathcal{O}},\vec{\mathcal{O}}^{\dagger})=\frac{1}{\pi^{2J}}\int\prod_{j=1}^{R}d^{2}w_{j}d^{2}w_{j}^{*}\penalty\ e^{-iw_{j}^{*}\mathcal{O}_{j}^{\dagger}}e^{-iw_{j}\mathcal{O}_{j}}\chi(\vec{w},\vec{w}^{*})

(26)

The dynamics of the positive-
{P}
distribution follow a Fokker-Planck equation that is equivalent to Eq. (
1
), while also employing normal-ordered cumulants as its natural dynamical variable set. We use this connection to first obtain an approximate Fokker-Planck equation for dynamics of the measurement chain in powers of the QNP nonlinearity. This directly allows us to obtain semi-analytic solutions for the TEOMs, Eqs. (
10
), (
11
), from which classification metrics such as the Fisher’s discriminant can be readily evaluated. Our analysis and its results, which are used throughout the main text, are detailed in the SI, Sec.
E
and
G
.

Appendix C
Scaling of QNP performance with nonlinearity for fixed
\mathcal{E}_{\rm eff}

In Sec.
III.1
, we explored how QNP parameters can be optimized to improve classification performance. However, a plain reading of the expression for the mean separation
\delta{\bm{\mu}}
, Eq. (
13
), would appear to suggest that an even simpler strategy may be to simply increase the QNP nonlinearity strength
\Lambda
, which monotonically scales
\delta{\bm{\mu}}
. However, in practice this dependence is much more complex: for a classification task defined by a given
\mathcal{A}
, varying
\Lambda
also varies
\mathcal{E}_{\rm eff}
via Eq. (
15
). This changes the operating conditions of the QNP and directly influences the mean separation and noise properties.

Figure 9:
\mathcal{C}_{\rm max}
as a function of QNP nonlinearity
{\Lambda}
for instances of Task I with different amplitudes
\mathcal{A}
as indicated, calculated using numerical integration of the STEOMs. For each task instance, the top panel shows
||\delta{\bm{\mu}}||
and
\sigma^{2(1)}_{\delta{\bm{\mu}}}
, calculated using the NVK approximation (solid curves), and STEOMs (dots).

Therefore, to observe and understand the scaling of
||\delta{\bm{\mu}}||
with nonlinearity, we must keep
\mathcal{E}_{\rm eff}
fixed while varying the nonlinearity. Practically, we do so by using QNPs of varying nonlinearity to perform separate instances of Task I, characterized by different values of
\mathcal{A}\cdot\frac{\Gamma_{1}}{\gamma}
. For each instance, the achieved
\mathcal{C}_{\rm max}
as a function of
\Lambda
is plotted in Fig.
9
.

We immediately note that for decreasing
\mathcal{A}\cdot\frac{\Gamma_{1}}{\gamma}
, the required QNP nonlinearity to reach the optimal
\mathcal{C}_{\rm max}
increases, as required by the form of
\mathcal{E}_{\rm eff}
. The crosses indicate three specific QNPs, one for each instance of the considered task, with nonlinearity
\Lambda
such that
\mathcal{E}_{\rm eff}
is the same. Comparing these QNPs, we now clearly see that the optimal
\mathcal{C}_{\rm max}
increases with increasing
\Lambda
. In the top panel of Fig.
9
, we plot the mean separation amplitude
||\delta{\bm{\mu}}||
and the projected noise
\sigma^{2(l)}_{\delta{\bm{\mu}}}
for
l=1
for each considered instance of Task I, under the NVK approximation and using integration of STEOMs. To lowest order in nonlinearity as captured by the NVK approximation, the projected noise properties remain the same for each instance. In contrast, the mean separation increases with nonlinearity, indicative of the sought-after scaling. Unchanged noise properties with increasing mean separation imply an increase in
D_{\rm F}
and hence classification accuracy.

Note that with increasing nonlinearity
\Lambda
, the agreement between the NVK approximation and exact integration of the STEOMs is reduced. In particular, the exact mean separation is lower than the NVK approximation, while the projected noise is larger. Both effects serve to reduce
D_{\rm F}
and hence limit the improvement in
\mathcal{C}_{\rm max}
to below that predicted by the NVK approximation. These observations are an example of saturation that is higher-order in the QNP nonlinearity and hence not captured by the NVK approximation, and are commonplace in strongly-driven nonlinear quantum systems
Boutin et al. 2017
.

Appendix D
Models of standard linear quantum amplifiers

In this section we provide the models for both phase-preserving and phase-sensitive linear quantum amplifiers deployed for readout in cQED, and which we use as benchmarks in Sec.
IV
of the main text. Note that our description of the measurement chain, Eq. (
1
), is general enough to include this standard paradigm, by neglecting the nonlinear contribution to the Hamiltonian,
\hat{\mathcal{N}}_{\rm QNP{}}\to 0
.

In particular, by taking
\hat{\mathcal{H}}_{\rm QNP{}}\to\hat{\mathcal{H}}_{\rm PP}
where

\displaystyle\hat{\mathcal{H}}_{\rm PP}=-\sum_{k}\Delta_{k}\hat{b}_{k}^{\dagger}\hat{b}_{k}+G_{\rm PP}(-i\hat{b}_{1}\hat{b}_{2}+h.c.),

(27)

we are able to describe QS readout using linear phase-preserving quantum amplifiers.

For phase-sensitive (PS) amplifiers on the other hand, we can set
\hat{\mathcal{H}}_{\rm QNP{}}\to\hat{\mathcal{H}}_{\rm PS}
, where

\displaystyle\hat{\mathcal{H}}_{\rm PS}=-\sum_{k}\Delta_{k}\hat{b}_{k}^{\dagger}\hat{b}_{k}+G_{\rm PS}(-i\hat{b}_{1}^{2}+h.c.).

(28)

Appendix E
Quantum Chernoff Bound: additional details

In this Appendix section we provide some additional details of the calculation of the Quantum Chernoff bound (QCB) used in Sec.
IV
of the main text to determine the optimal discrimination bounds for quantum state discrimination tasks.

To define the QCB as used in the main text, we first introduce the quantity
Audenaert et al. 2007
;
Calsamiglia et al. 2008
:

\displaystyle Q(\hat{\rho}^{(l)},\hat{\rho}^{(p)})=-\min_{s\in[0,1]}\log{\rm tr}\left[(\hat{\rho}^{(l)})^{s}(\hat{\rho}^{(p)})^{1-s}\right]

(29)

We compute the QCB using only the quantum states of the modes that are intended to be monitored. For Task I, this is simply mode
\hat{a}_{1}
for the QS. We can then define the QCB for the QS
\zeta_{\rm QS}
as:

\displaystyle\zeta_{\rm QS}\equiv Q\left({\rm tr}_{\hat{a}_{2}}\!\!\left[\hat{\rho}^{(1)}_{\rm QS}\right],{\rm tr}_{\hat{a}_{2}}\!\!\left[\hat{\rho}^{(2)}_{\rm QS}\right]\right)

(30)

where
{\rm tr}_{\hat{o}}[\hat{\rho}]
is used to trace out the sector corresponding to mode
\hat{o}
from the density matrix
\hat{\rho}
. For the general quantum processor, only mode
\hat{b}_{1}
is monitored, so the QCB
\zeta
analogously becomes:

\displaystyle\zeta\equiv Q\left({\rm tr}_{\hat{b}_{2}}\!\!\left[\hat{\rho}^{(1)}_{\rm QNP{}}\right],{\rm tr}_{\hat{b}_{2}}\!\!\left[\hat{\rho}^{(2)}_{\rm QNP{}}\right]\right)

(31)

Note that the QNP and the PS amplifier are both single-mode devices for Task I, so the trace operation has no effect in those cases.

The expressions in Eqs. (
30
), (
31
) can be computed straightforwardly if the quantum states are Gaussian. These simplified expressions are derived in Ref.
Calsamiglia et al. 2008
; we employ these results in plotting the QCB in the main text, Fig.
5
.

Appendix F
Non-classical noise due to the QNP alone: classifying thermal states

In the main text, the considered Tasks I and II both require classification of states that exhibit some degree of quantum correlations, namely at least one state exhibits some degree of single or two-mode squeezing. As a result, the observed quantum (i.e. sub-vacuum) noise observed at the QNP output will in general contain a contribution attributable to the quantum correlations of QS signals, and cannot be attributed to the QNP alone.

In this Appendix section, we show that non-classical correlations can be generated solely by appropriate QNP models, and they can be manipulated for use in classification in the same way as has been demonstrated for both Task I and II. To this end, we first introduce the modified Liouvillian for the QS:

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{\rm QS}\hat{\rho}

\displaystyle=-i[\hat{\mathcal{H}}^{(l)}_{\rm QS},\hat{\rho}]+

\displaystyle\penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \sum_{m}\kappa_{m}(n^{{\rm th}(l)}_{m}+1)\mathcal{D}[\hat{a}_{m}]\hat{\rho}+\kappa_{m}n^{{\rm th}(l)}_{m}\mathcal{D}[\hat{a}^{\dagger}_{m}]\hat{\rho},

(32)

where in contrast to Eq. (
2
) each QS mode is now coupled to a finite temperature bath. For simplicity, we further restrict ourselves to a single-mode QS being monitored by a single-mode QNP,
M=K=1
; all QS parameters for mode
m=2
are set to zero. Secondly, we turn off single-mode squeezing of the QS mode,
G_{1}^{(l)}=0\penalty\ \forall\penalty\ l
, so that the QS states to be distinguished have no non-trivial quantum correlations. Instead, the states to be distinguished are characterized entirely by different thermal bath temperatures and hence occupation numbers,
n^{{\rm th}(l)}_{m}
. While not straightforward to generate in practice, this choice is ideal to test whether non-classical correlations can be generated by the QNP alone, and whether they can still be manipulated by the QNP. All parameters characterizing Task III are summarized in Table
3
.

Table 3:
QS parameters (
M=1
only) defining the third classification task considered, in units of QS mode total loss rate
\kappa
.

Task III

\mathcal{A}

Figure 10:
QNP performance metrics for Task III, classification of thermal states, within the NVK approximation. (a) Mean separation
||\delta{\bm{\mu}}||
and (b) noise properties as a function of nonlinearity
{\Lambda}/\gamma
. The reduction of projected noise to below vacuum levels where input signals are classical (originating from a thermal state) indicates that the QNP alone can give rise to non-classical correlations in the measured outputs.

It suffices to consider only the NVK approximation and to then plot both the the mean separation of measured QNP quadratures
||\delta{\bm{\mu}}||
and the projected noise in Fig.
10
, as a function of QNP nonlinearity. We note that the qualitative features are as observed in Fig.
4
, and therefore reiterate the main results in the paper: the ability to compute correlations
in situ
, and the ability to control noise in measured quadratures differently from their mean value. Crucially, projected noise below vacuum in Fig.
10
(b) can now solely be attributed to the action of the QNP, as the QS states are purely thermal and do not demonstrate such non-classical correlations; in fact, they exhibit fluctuations above the vacuum value due to the coupling to a finite temperature bath. This emphasizes that the QNP can provide the ability to not just manipulate existing quantum correlations, but also provide useful quantum correlations itself.

We emphasize that the QNP model we consider here is based on coherently-driven Kerr oscillators, which by themselves are not ideal squeezers. However, our general approach applies to much more general QNP models, where more non-classical correlations could be generated.

Appendix B
Quantum cumulants

The use of quantum cumulants as an efficient representation of certain QNP states is essential to our work. In this section, we provide a simplified introduction to quantum cumulants as a description of quantum states.

B.1
Definition in terms of the generating function

The starting point for our discussion of cumulants is via the characteristic function
\chi(z,z^{*})
, defined in terms of the density operator
\hat{\rho}
for an arbitrary quantum state as

\displaystyle\chi(z,z^{*})={\rm tr}\left\{\hat{\rho}e^{iz^{*}\hat{b}^{\dagger}}e^{iz\hat{b}}\right\}.

(33)

The characteristic function as defined in Eq. (
33
) has the property that it defines all
normal-ordered
operator averages with respect to the quantum state
\hat{\rho}
; simply evaluating the derivative of the characteristic function
Carmichael 2002
at
z=z^{*}=0
yields

\left.\frac{\partial^{p+q}}{\partial(iz^{*})^{p}\partial(iz)^{q}}(\mathrm{tr}\{\rho e^{iz^{*}\hat{b}^{\dagger}}e^{iz\hat{b}}\})\right|_{z=z^{*}=0}=\langle\hat{b}^{\dagger p}\hat{b}^{q}\rangle.

(34)

It is then possible to define a distribution function for calculating these normal-ordered moments,

\displaystyle P(\beta,\beta^{\dagger})

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\pi^{2}}\int d^{2}z\penalty\ \chi(z,z^{\ast})e^{-iz^{\ast}\beta^{\ast}}e^{-iz\beta}

(35)

for which we use the suggestive notation
P(\beta,\beta^{\dagger})
, as it is easily shown to be none other than the Glauber-Sudarshan
P
distribution
Glauber 1963
;
Sudarshan 1963
;
Carmichael 2002
:

\displaystyle\hat{\rho}=\int d^{2}\beta\penalty\ P(\beta,\beta^{\dagger})|\beta\rangle\langle\beta|

(36)

Having recalled the connection of the characteristic function to the quantum state defined by
\hat{\rho}
and its normal-ordered moments, we now define a quantum generating function
G:\mathbb{C}\times\mathbb{C}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
,

G(z,z^{\ast})=\ln\mathrm{tr}\{\chi(z,z^{*})\}=\ln\mathrm{tr}\{\hat{\rho}e^{iz^{\ast}\hat{b}^{\dagger}}e^{iz\hat{b}}\}.

(37)

The
quantum cumulants
are defined through Eq. (
37
) by the series expansion of the generating function
G(z,z^{\ast})
which yields all quantum cumulants
C_{a^{\dagger p}a^{q}}
for any
p,q\in\mathbb{N}^{2}

\ln\mathrm{tr}\{\hat{\rho}e^{iz^{\ast}\hat{b}^{\dagger}}e^{iz\hat{b}}\}=\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}\!\!\sum_{\scriptsize{\begin{array}[]{c}p,q\in\mathbb{N}\\
p\!+\!q\!=\!n\end{array}}}\frac{1}{p!q!}C_{b^{\dagger p}b^{q}}(iz^{\ast})^{p}(iz)^{q},

(38)

or equivalently,

C_{b^{\dagger p}b^{q}}\equiv\left.\frac{\partial^{p+q}G}{\partial(iz^{\ast})^{p}\partial(iz)^{q}}(z,z^{\ast})\right|_{z=z^{\ast}=0}

(39)

So far, cumulants may simply appear to be a mathematical construct. However, it can be shown that normal-ordered cumulants and normal-ordered moments can be mapped to each other one-to-one. To see this, we can think of
G(z,z^{\ast})
as the composite of the logarithm function
\mathrm{log}(\chi)=\ln\chi
and
\chi(z,z^{*})={\rm tr}\left\{\hat{\rho}e^{iz^{*}\hat{b}^{\dagger}}e^{iz\hat{b}}\right\}
, and employ multi-variable version of Faà di Bruno’s formula for the
n
-th order partial derivative where
n=p+q
,

\displaystyle\frac{\partial^{p+q}G}{\partial(iz^{\ast})^{p}\partial(iz)^{q}}(z,z^{\ast})=\penalty\ \sum_{\pi}\left(\mathrm{log}^{(|\pi|)}(\chi(z,z^{\ast}))\prod_{B\in\pi}\frac{\partial^{p^{\prime}+q^{\prime}}\chi}{\partial(iz^{\ast})^{p^{\prime}}\partial(iz)^{q^{\prime}}}(z,z^{\ast})\right),

(40)

where
\pi
defines the set of possible partitions of the ordered set
\{iz^{\ast},\cdots,iz^{\ast},iz,\cdots iz\}
with
iz^{\ast}
appearing
p
times and
iz
appearing
q
times.
B
indicates elements in this set of partitions, and
p^{\prime}
and
q^{\prime}
respectively counts the number of
iz^{\ast}
and
iz
in each partition element
B
. Finally,
f^{(|\pi|)}(\cdot)
denotes the
|\pi|
-order derivative of the function
f(\cdot)
, where
|\pi|
denotes the cardinality of the set
\pi
. By noting that
\chi(0,0)=\mathrm{tr}(\hat{\rho})=1
, and then using
\mathrm{log}^{(|\pi|)}(\chi(0,0))=(\ln\chi)^{(|\pi|)}|_{\chi=1}=(-1)^{|\pi|-1}(|\pi|-1)!
we obtain

\displaystyle C_{b^{\dagger p}b^{q}}

\displaystyle=\left.\frac{\partial^{p+q}G}{\partial(iz^{\ast})^{p}\partial(iz)^{q}}(z,z^{\ast})\right|_{z=z^{\ast}=0}

\displaystyle=\sum_{\pi}\left((-1)^{|\pi|-1}(|\pi|-1)!\prod_{B\in\pi}\langle\hat{b}^{\dagger p^{\prime}}\hat{b}^{q^{\prime}}\rangle\!\right).

(41)

As such, cumulants can be thought of as a reparameterization of the normal-ordered moments that typically appear in observables measured in an experiment. We will see in the next two appendices that this reparameterization leads to a much more efficient description of certain quantum states, which can then be leveraged to construct an efficient computational framework.

For our purposes, we will find it more useful to express quantum moments in terms of quantum cumulants. This inverse transformation can be performed by thinking of
\chi(z,z^{*})={\rm tr}\left\{\hat{\rho}e^{iz^{*}\hat{b}^{\dagger}}e^{iz\hat{b}}\right\}
as the composite of the exponential function
\mathrm{exp}(G)=e^{G}
and
G(z,z^{\ast})=\ln\mathrm{tr}\{\hat{\rho}e^{iz^{\ast}\hat{b}^{\dagger}}e^{iz\hat{b}}\}
. Again, Faà di Bruno’s formula gives

\displaystyle\frac{\partial^{p+q}\chi}{\partial(iz^{\ast})^{p}\partial(iz)^{q}}(z,z^{*})=\sum_{\pi}\!\!\left(\mathrm{exp}^{(|\pi|)}(G(z,z^{*}))\prod_{B\in\pi}\frac{\partial^{p^{\prime}+q^{\prime}}G}{\partial(iz^{\ast})^{p^{\prime}}\partial(iz)^{q^{\prime}}}(z,z^{*})\!\right)

(42)

where
\pi
defines the set of possible partitions of the ordered set
\{iz^{\ast},\cdots,iz^{\ast},iz,\cdots iz\}
with
iz^{\ast}
appearing
p
times and
iz
appearing
q
times,
B
indicates elements in this set of partitions, and
p^{\prime}
and
q^{\prime}
respectively counts the number of
iz^{\ast}
and
iz
in each partition element
B
. Again, noting that
G(0,0)=\ln\mathrm{tr}(\hat{\rho})=0
, and then using
\mathrm{exp}^{(|\pi|)}(G(0,0))=(e^{G})^{(|\pi|)}|_{G=0}=1
we get for
n
-th order partial derivative where
n=p+q
:

\displaystyle\langle\hat{b}^{\dagger p}\hat{b}^{q}\rangle

\displaystyle=\left.\frac{\partial^{p+q}\chi}{\partial(iz^{\ast})^{p}\partial(iz)^{q}}(z,z^{\ast})\right|_{z=z^{\ast}=0}

\displaystyle=\left.\sum_{\pi}\prod_{B\in\pi}\frac{\partial^{p^{\prime}+q^{\prime}}G}{\partial(iz^{\ast})^{p^{\prime}}\partial(iz)^{q^{\prime}}}(z,z^{\ast})\right|_{z=z^{\ast}=0}

\displaystyle=\sum_{\pi}\prod_{B\in\pi}C_{b^{\dagger p^{\prime}}b^{q^{\prime}}}.

(43)

Finally, it is straightforward to generalize the definition of quantum cumulants to the bosonic
N
-mode case. For a complex-valued vector
\bm{z}=(z_{1},z_{2},\cdots,z_{N})
, we can define the generating function
G:\mathbb{C}^{N}\times\mathbb{C}^{N}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
as:

\displaystyle G(\bm{z},\bm{z}^{\ast})=\ln\mathrm{tr}\left\{\hat{\rho}e^{iz^{\ast}_{1}\hat{b}_{1}^{\dagger}}\cdots e^{iz^{\ast}_{N}\hat{b}_{N}^{\dagger}}e^{iz_{1}\hat{b}_{1}}\cdots e^{iz_{N}\hat{b}_{N}}\right\}.

(44)

The series expansion of
G(\bm{z},\bm{z}^{\ast})
yields all quantum cumulants

\displaystyle\left.\frac{\partial^{n}G}{\partial(iz_{1}^{\ast})^{p_{1}}\!\cdots\!\partial(iz_{N}^{\ast})^{p_{N}}\partial(iz_{1})^{q_{1}}\!\cdots\!\partial(iz_{N})^{q_{N}}}(\bm{z},\bm{z}^{\ast})\right|_{\bm{z}=\bm{z}^{\ast}=\bm{0}}=C_{b_{1}^{\dagger p_{1}}\cdots b_{N}^{\dagger p_{N}}b_{1}^{q_{1}}\cdots b_{N}^{q_{N}}}.

(45)

where
p_{1}+\cdots+p_{N}+q_{1}+\cdots+q_{N}=n
. The transformation between moments and cumulants in multi-mode case can be obtained by simply replacing all single-mode moments and cumulants in Eq.(
41
), (
43
) with multi-mode moments and cumulants corresponding to normal-ordered operator
\hat{o}_{1}\hat{o}_{2}\cdots\hat{o}_{n}=\hat{b}_{1}^{\dagger p_{1}}\cdots\hat{b}_{N}^{\dagger p_{N}}\hat{b}_{1}^{q_{1}}\cdots\hat{b}_{N}^{q_{N}}
:

\displaystyle C_{o_{1}o_{2}\cdots o_{n}}

\displaystyle=\sum_{\pi}\left((-1)^{|\pi|-1}(|\pi|-1)!\prod_{B\in\pi}\langle\hat{o}_{i}:i\in B\rangle\right),

(46)

\displaystyle\langle\hat{o}_{1}\hat{o}_{2}\cdots\hat{o}_{n}\rangle

\displaystyle=\sum_{\pi}\prod_{B\in\pi}C_{o_{i}:i\in B},

(47)

where
\pi
defines the set of possible partitions of operators in the
n
-order moment, and
B
indicates elements in this set of partitions.

B.2
Cumulants for some simple quantum states

Eqs. (
41
), (
43
) and their multi-mode generalization Eqs. (
46
), (
47
) show that quantum cumulants and quantum moments have a one-to-one correspondence through logarithm and exponential. On the other hand, for two complex-valued vectors
\bm{z}=(z_{1},z_{2},\cdots,z_{N})
and
\bm{\beta}=(\beta_{1},\beta_{2},\cdots,\beta_{N})
, the
P
-representation of quantum state and quantum characteristic function are mutually related by Fourier transformation
Carmichael 2002
:

\displaystyle P(\bm{\beta},\bm{\beta}^{\ast})

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\pi^{2}}\int\chi(\bm{z},\bm{z}^{\ast})e^{-i\bm{z}\cdot\bm{\beta}^{\ast}}e^{-i\bm{z}^{\ast}\bm{\beta}}\penalty\ d^{2}\bm{z},

(48)

\displaystyle\chi(\bm{z},\bm{z}^{\ast})

\displaystyle=\int P(\bm{\beta},\bm{\beta}^{\ast})e^{i\bm{z}\cdot\bm{\beta}^{\ast}}e^{i\bm{z}^{\ast}\bm{\beta}}\penalty\ d^{2}\bm{\beta}.

(49)

where the inner product is conventionally
\bm{z}\cdot\bm{\beta}^{\ast}=z^{\ast}_{1}\beta_{1}^{\ast}+\cdots+z^{\ast}_{N}\beta_{N}^{\ast}
and
\bm{z}^{\ast}\cdot\bm{\beta}=z_{1}\beta_{1}+\cdots+z_{N}\beta_{N}
. This result ensures the one-to-one correspondence between a quantum state and the quantum cumulants.

Importantly, the truncation of cumulants to certain discrete orders naturally characterizes some special types of quantum states. In this section, we will show that states with only nonzero first order cumulants correspond to coherent states, while states with only nonzero first and second cumulants orders correspond to Gaussian states.

We first consider a product of coherent states
\ket{\bm{\beta}_0}\equiv\ket{\beta_1, \beta_2, \cdots, \beta_N}
. In Eq. (
44
), we then set
\hat{\rho}=\ket{\boldsymbol{\beta}_0}\bra{\boldsymbol{\beta}_0}
, following which the RHS gives

\displaystyle\ln\mathrm{tr}\left\{\ket{\boldsymbol{\beta}_0}\bra{\boldsymbol{\beta}_0}e^{iz^{\ast}_{1}\hat{b}_{1}^{\dagger}}\cdots e^{iz^{\ast}_{N}\hat{b}_{N}^{\dagger}}e^{iz_{1}\hat{b}_{1}}\cdots e^{iz_{N}\hat{b}_{N}}\right\}

\displaystyle=\penalty\

\displaystyle\ln\left(\bra{\boldsymbol{\beta}_0}e^{iz^{\ast}_{1}\hat{b}_{1}^{\dagger}}\cdots e^{iz^{\ast}_{N}\hat{b}_{N}^{\dagger}}e^{iz_{1}\hat{b}_{1}}\cdots e^{iz_{N}\hat{b}_{N}}\ket{\boldsymbol{\beta}_0}\right)

\displaystyle=\penalty\

\displaystyle\ln e^{iz^{\ast}_{1}\beta_{1}^{\ast}+\cdots+iz^{\ast}_{N}\beta_{N}^{\ast}+iz_{1}\beta_{1}+\cdots+iz_{N}\beta_{N}}

\displaystyle=\penalty\

\displaystyle iz^{\ast}_{1}\beta_{1}^{\ast}+\cdots+iz^{\ast}_{N}\beta_{N}^{\ast}+iz_{1}\beta_{1}+\cdots+iz_{N}\beta_{N},

(50)

From Eq. (
45
), we obtain non-trivial derivative contributions only for first-order derivatives. Thus for all
k\in\{1,2,\cdots,N\}
, we have
C_{b_{k}}=\beta_{k}
and
C_{b_{k}^{\dagger}}=\beta_{k}^{\ast}
, so that

\displaystyle\left(C_{b_{1}},C_{b_{2}},\cdots,C_{b_{N}}\right)

\displaystyle=\bm{\beta}_{0},

(51a)

\displaystyle\left(C_{b^{\dagger}_{1}},C_{b^{\dagger}_{2}},\cdots,C_{b^{\dagger}_{N}}\right)

\displaystyle=\bm{\beta}^{\ast}_{0}

(51b)

We can also show that physical states with vanishing cumulants of order greater than one are necessarily coherent states. We begin with the normal-ordered characteristic function for such states, which is given by
\chi(\bm{z},\bm{z}^{\ast})=e^{G(i\bm{z}^{\ast},i\bm{z})}=e^{i\bm{z}\cdot\bm{\beta}^{\ast}_{0}+i\bm{z}^{\ast}\cdot\bm{\beta}_{0}}
. The corresponding
P
-representation is simply the Fourier transform of the characteristic function, and is given by

\displaystyle P(\bm{\beta},\bm{\beta}^{\ast})

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\pi^{2}}\int\chi(\bm{z},\bm{z}^{\ast})e^{-i\bm{z}\cdot\bm{\beta}^{\ast}}e^{-i\bm{z}^{\ast}\cdot\bm{\beta}}\penalty\ d^{2}\bm{z}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\pi^{2}}\int e^{i\bm{z}\cdot\bm{\beta}^{\ast}_{0}+i\bm{z}^{\ast}\cdot\bm{\beta}_{0}}e^{-i\bm{z}\cdot\bm{\beta}^{\ast}}e^{-i\bm{z}^{\ast}\cdot\bm{\beta}}\penalty\ d^{2}\bm{z}

\displaystyle=\delta(\bm{\beta}-\bm{\beta}_{0}),

(52)

which describes a product of coherent states. As a result, the states with all cumulants or order greater than one vanishing are exactly the collection of all coherent states.

Next, we consider quantum states whose quasi-probability distribution is Gaussian. Recall that the
P
,
Q
, and Wigner phase-space representations are related by the Weierstrass transformation, which always maps one Gaussian distribution to another Gaussian distribution
Carmichael 2002
:

\displaystyle W(\bm{\beta},\bm{\beta}^{\ast})

\displaystyle=\frac{2}{\pi}\int d^{2}\bm{\widetilde{\beta}}\penalty\ e^{-2|\bm{\beta}-\bm{\widetilde{\beta}}|^{2}}P(\bm{\widetilde{\beta}},\bm{\widetilde{\beta}}^{\ast}),

(53)

\displaystyle Q(\bm{\beta},\bm{\beta}^{\ast})

\displaystyle=\frac{2}{\pi}\int d^{2}\bm{\widetilde{\beta}}\penalty\ e^{-2|\bm{\beta}-\bm{\widetilde{\beta}}|^{2}}W(\bm{\widetilde{\beta}},\bm{\widetilde{\beta}}^{\ast}).

(54)

The relation between the
P
-representation and characteristic function in Eqs. (
48
), (
49
) respectively is via Fourier transform, which also maps one Gaussian distribution to another Gaussian distribution.

Via this series of transformations, we therefore see that a quantum state with a Gaussian Wigner distribution will also have a Gaussian characteristic function, and hence must be entirely defined by cumulants of up to second-order (by definition of a Gaussian distribution).

Appendix C
Truncated Equations of Motion approach applied to the single-mode Kerr QNP

To benchmark the computational approach developed in this paper based on quantum cumulants, we will use a simple system as a testbed: a single coherently-drive Kerr oscillator. This system also forms a single mode of the QNPs we employ.

The resulting system schematic is shown in Fig.
11
. We begin by analyzing the
unconditional
dynamics of the resulting system, described by the quantum master equation

\displaystyle d\hat{\rho}\equiv\mathcal{L}\hat{\rho}\penalty\ dt=\mathcal{L}_{\rm QNP}\hat{\rho}\penalty\ dt-i[\hat{\mathcal{H}}_{d},\hat{\rho}]\penalty\ dt

(55)

where the drive Hamiltonian in the frame rotating at the drive frequency
\omega_{d}
is then

\displaystyle\hat{\mathcal{H}}_{d}=\eta_{b}(\hat{b}+\hat{b}^{\dagger}),

(56)

and we have defined for convenience
\hat{b}\equiv\hat{b}_{1}
. The coherent drive is incident on the QNP directly, via a separate input port with loss rate
\Gamma_{1}
. This situation describes QNP processing of classical signals encoded in the generally time-dependent coherent drive amplitude and phase. The Liouvillian
\mathcal{L}_{\rm QRC}
describing the single-mode QNP takes the form

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{\rm QNP}\hat{\rho}=-i\left[-\Delta_{1}\hat{b}^{\dagger}\hat{b}-\frac{\Lambda_{1}}{2}\hat{b}^{\dagger}\hat{b}^{\dagger}\hat{b}\hat{b},\hat{\rho}\right]+(\gamma_{1}+\Gamma_{1})\mathcal{D}[\hat{b}]\hat{\rho}

(57)

where
\Delta=\omega_{d}-\omega_{1}
is the detuning of the drive frequency from the bare QNP mode frequency
\omega
. For simplicity of notation, in what follows we introduce for convenience the total linear damping
{\gamma}=\gamma_{1}+\Gamma_{1}
of the nonlinear mode.

Figure 11:
Schematic showing a single-mode QNP as part of a simplified measurement chain: a single frequency coherent drive is incident on the QNP via an input channel that is not measured, while the second port of the QNP is monitored. The QNP can be realized in cQED as a tunable-frequency Kerr oscillator using a capacitively shunted SQUID loop
Koch et al. 2007
.

C.1
Obtaining TEOMs for a single coherently-driven Kerr oscillator

Our approach requires the expression of moments of a multimode quantum system in terms of their associated cumulants. Moments and cumulants are related via their generating functions, which allows one to write a general prescription for writing an arbitrary normal-ordered joint system moment of order
n
in terms of a series of cumulants of order
1
up to
n
. This takes the specific form given by Eq. (
47
),

\displaystyle\langle\hat{o}_{1}\hat{o}_{2}\ldots\hat{o}_{n}\rangle=\sum_{\pi}\prod_{B\in\pi}C_{o_{i}:i\in B},

(58)

where
\pi
defines the set of possible partitions of operators in the
n
-order moment, and
B
indicates elements in this set of partitions. The rather opaque form can be simplified by considering a specific case. We will use the above prescription to obtain the TEOMs for the single-mode QNP.

This minimal system already includes the key nonlinear element of the QNP, and therefore the most complex terms that need to be analyzed will arise already in these equations. We start by writing down the equation for
\langle\hat{b}\rangle={\rm tr}\{\mathcal{L}\hat{\rho}\hat{b}\}
,

\displaystyle\langle\dot{\hat{b}}\rangle=\left(i\Delta_{1}-\frac{{\gamma}}{2}\right)\langle\hat{b}\rangle+i\Lambda_{1}\langle\hat{b}^{\dagger}\hat{b}\hat{b}\rangle-i\eta_{b}.

(59)

To rewrite the above equation in terms of cumulants, we must express the third-order moment
\langle\hat{b}^{\dagger}\hat{b}\hat{b}\rangle
in terms of cumulants. The possible partitions of the third-order moment are given by:
\pi\in\{(\hat{b}^{\dagger}\hat{b}\hat{b}),(\hat{b}^{\dagger}\hat{b},\hat{b})\times 2,(\hat{b}\hat{b},\hat{b}^{\dagger}),(\hat{b}^{\dagger},\hat{b},\hat{b})\}
. Then, using Eq. (
58
), we obtain

\displaystyle\langle\hat{b}^{\dagger}\hat{b}\hat{b}\rangle=C_{b^{\dagger}bb}+2C_{b^{\dagger}b}\langle\hat{b}\rangle+C_{bb}\langle\hat{b}^{\dagger}\rangle+\langle\hat{b}^{\dagger}\rangle\langle\hat{b}\rangle^{2}.

(60)

Using the above, we can now rewrite Eq. (
59
) in terms of cumulants as

\displaystyle\langle\dot{\hat{b}}\rangle=\left(i\Delta_{1}-\frac{{\gamma}}{2}\right)\langle\hat{b}\rangle+i\Lambda_{1}\langle\hat{b}^{\dagger}\rangle\langle\hat{b}\rangle^{2}-i\eta_{b}+i\Lambda_{1}\left(C_{bb}\langle\hat{b}^{\dagger}\rangle+2C_{b^{\dagger}b}\langle\hat{b}\rangle+C_{b^{\dagger}bb}\right)

(61)

To similarly obtain dynamical equations for cumulants
C_{b^{\dagger}b},C_{bb}
, we write equations of motion for the second-order moments
\langle\hat{b}^{\dagger}\hat{b}\rangle
and
\langle\hat{b}\hat{b}\rangle
respectively. Starting with the former, we find:

\displaystyle\frac{d}{dt}\langle\hat{b}^{\dagger}\hat{b}\rangle

\displaystyle=-{\gamma}\langle\hat{b}^{\dagger}\hat{b}\rangle+i\eta_{b}\langle\hat{b}\rangle-i\eta_{b}\langle\hat{b}^{\dagger}\rangle

\displaystyle=-{\gamma}C_{b^{\dagger}b}-{\gamma}\langle\hat{b}^{\dagger}\rangle\langle\hat{b}\rangle+i\eta_{b}\langle\hat{b}\rangle-i\eta_{b}\langle\hat{b}^{\dagger}\rangle

(62)

where in the second line we rewrite the second-order moment in terms of cumulants. From this we can easily write
dC_{b^{\dagger}b}=d\langle\hat{b}^{\dagger}\hat{b}\rangle-(d\langle\hat{b}^{\dagger}\rangle)\langle\hat{b}\rangle-(d\langle\hat{b}\rangle)\langle\hat{b}^{\dagger}\rangle
, which yields the dynamical equation for
C_{b^{\dagger}b}
:

\displaystyle\dot{C}_{b^{\dagger}b}=-{\gamma}C_{b^{\dagger}b}-i\Lambda_{1}\left(C_{bb}\langle\hat{b}^{\dagger}\rangle^{2}-C_{bb}^{*}\langle\hat{b}\rangle^{2}\right)-i\Lambda_{1}\left(C_{b^{\dagger}bb}\langle\hat{b}^{\dagger}\rangle-C_{b^{\dagger}bb}^{*}\langle\hat{b}\rangle\right)

(63)

Finally, we write the equation of motion for
\langle\hat{b}\hat{b}\rangle
,

\displaystyle\frac{d}{dt}\langle\hat{b}\hat{b}\rangle

\displaystyle=\left(2i\Delta_{1}-{\gamma}\right)\langle\hat{b}\hat{b}\rangle-2i\eta_{b}\langle\hat{b}\rangle+i\Lambda_{1}\langle\hat{b}\hat{b}\rangle+i2\Lambda_{1}\langle\hat{b}^{\dagger}\hat{b}\hat{b}\hat{b}\rangle

\displaystyle=\left(2i\Delta_{1}-{\gamma}+i\Lambda_{1}\right)(C_{bb}+\langle\hat{b}\rangle\langle\hat{b}\rangle)-2i\eta_{b}\langle\hat{b}\rangle+i2\Lambda_{1}\langle\hat{b}^{\dagger}\hat{b}\hat{b}\hat{b}\rangle

(64)

where we once again rewrite second-order moments in terms of cumulants in the second line. We now need to express the remaining fourth-order moment
\langle\hat{b}^{\dagger}\hat{b}\hat{b}\hat{b}\rangle
in terms of cumulants. The possible partitions of this fourth-order moment are given by
\pi\in\{(\hat{b}^{\dagger}\hat{b}\hat{b}\hat{b}),(\hat{b}^{\dagger}\hat{b}\hat{b},\hat{b})\times 3,(\hat{b}\hat{b}\hat{b},\hat{b}^{\dagger}),(\hat{b}^{\dagger}\hat{b},\hat{b}\hat{b})\times 3,(\hat{b}^{\dagger}\hat{b},\hat{b},\hat{b})\times 3,(\hat{b}\hat{b},\hat{b}^{\dagger},\hat{b})\times 3,(\hat{b}^{\dagger},\hat{b},\hat{b},\hat{b})\}
, so that

\displaystyle\langle\hat{b}^{\dagger}\hat{b}\hat{b}\hat{b}\rangle

\displaystyle=C_{{b}^{\dagger}{b}{b}{b}}+3\langle\hat{b}\rangle C_{b^{\dagger}bb}+\langle\hat{b}^{\dagger}\rangle C_{bbb}

\displaystyle+3C_{b^{\dagger}b}C_{bb}+3C_{b^{\dagger}b}\langle\hat{b}\rangle^{2}+3C_{bb}|\langle\hat{b}\rangle|^{2}+\langle\hat{b}^{\dagger}\rangle\langle\hat{b}\rangle\langle\hat{b}\rangle\langle\hat{b}\rangle

(65)

From this we can finally write
dC_{bb}=d\langle\hat{b}\hat{b}\rangle-2(d\langle\hat{b}\rangle)\langle\hat{b}\rangle
, which yields the dynamical equation for
C_{bb}
:

\displaystyle\dot{C}_{bb}

\displaystyle=\left(2i\Delta_{1}-{\gamma}+i\Lambda_{1}\right)(C_{bb}+\langle\hat{b}\rangle^{2})-2i\eta_{b}\langle\hat{b}\rangle

\displaystyle\penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ +i2\Lambda_{1}\left(C_{{b}^{\dagger}{b}{b}{b}}+3\langle\hat{b}\rangle C_{b^{\dagger}bb}+\langle\hat{b}^{\dagger}\rangle C_{bbb}\right)

\displaystyle\penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ +i2\Lambda_{1}\left(3C_{b^{\dagger}b}C_{bb}+3C_{b^{\dagger}b}\langle\hat{b}\rangle^{2}+3C_{bb}|\langle\hat{b}\rangle|^{2}+\langle\hat{b}^{\dagger}\rangle\langle\hat{b}\rangle^{3}\right)

\displaystyle\penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ -2\left(i\Delta_{1}-\frac{{\gamma}}{2}\right)\langle\hat{b}\rangle^{2}-i2\Lambda_{1}\langle\hat{b}^{\dagger}\rangle\langle\hat{b}\rangle^{3}+2i\eta_{b}\langle\hat{b}\rangle

\displaystyle\penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ -i2\Lambda_{1}\left(C_{bb}|\langle\hat{b}\rangle|^{2}+2C_{b^{\dagger}b}\langle\hat{b}\rangle^{2}+C_{b^{\dagger}bb}\langle\hat{b}\rangle\right)

(66)

which finally simplifies to:

\displaystyle\dot{C}_{bb}=\left(2i\Delta_{1}-{\gamma}+i\Lambda_{1}\right)C_{bb}+i\Lambda_{1}\langle\hat{b}\rangle^{2}+i4\Lambda_{1}|\langle\hat{b}\rangle|^{2}C_{bb}+i6\Lambda_{1}C_{b^{\dagger}b}C_{bb}

\displaystyle+i2\Lambda_{1}C_{b^{\dagger}b}\langle\hat{b}\rangle^{2}+i2\Lambda_{1}C_{b^{\dagger}bbb}+i4\Lambda_{1}C_{b^{\dagger}bb}\langle\hat{b}\rangle+i2\Lambda_{1}C_{bbb}\langle\hat{b}^{\dagger}\rangle

(67)

Clearly, second-order cumulants couple to cumulants of third and fourth order. This is a general feature of nonlinear quantum systems: moments and thus cumulants of a certain order can couple to those of higher-order, leading to an infinite hierarchy of equations that do not form a closed set.

However, an infinite set of normal-ordered cumulants is not necessarily required to describe all multimode quantum states. In particular, as we have seen in Sec.
B
of the SI, a multimode quantum system in a product of coherent states is described entirely by its nonzero first-order cumulants; all cumulants of order
p+q>1
vanish. Multimode quantum states that are defined entirely by their first and second-order cumulants admit Gaussian phase-space representations, and are labelled Gaussian states. States with nonzero cumulants of third or higher-order are thus by definition non-Gaussian states
Boutin et al. 2017
;
Ra et al. 2020
.

Our numerical approach leverages this efficient representation of specific multimode quantum states in terms of cumulants. In particular, we consider an ansatz wherein the quantum state of the complete measurement is described entirely by cumulants up to a finite order
p+q\leq n_{\rm trunc}
; all cumulants of order
p+q>n_{\rm trunc}
are thus set to zero, truncating the hierarchy and yielding a closed set of equations for the retained nonzero cumulants. In this work, we choose
n_{\rm trunc}=2
for a quantum measurement chain defined entirely by its first and second-order cumulants, although the truncation can similarly be carried out at higher order. The resulting Truncated Equations Of Motion (TEOMs), and their generalization to
conditional
dynamics of multimode quantum systems, the Stochastic Truncate Equations of Motion (STEOMs), form the basis of our computational approach in this paper. We have developed a computer algebra approach that automates the above process of calculating contributions to equations of motion and expressing arbitrary moments in terms of cumulants, thus allowing the systematic truncation necessary to arrive at TEOMs (and STEOMs) introduced in the main text.

C.2
Calculating STEOMs - stochastic measurement contributions to truncated cumulant dynamics

The measurement-conditioned dynamics of a quantum system under heterodyne monitoring is given by a stochastic master equation (SME). We can now calculate the contributions to equations of motion for cumulants due to stochastic contributions via an SME. To illustrate the salient features of the calculation, a single homodyne measurement superoperator for monitoring a general mode
\hat{b}_{k}
suffices:

\displaystyle\mathcal{S}_{\rm hom}[\sqrt{\gamma_{k}}\hat{b}_{k}]\hat{\rho}=\sqrt{\frac{\gamma_{k}}{2}}\left(\hat{b}_{k}^{\ }\hat{\rho}+\hat{\rho}\hat{b}_{k}^{\dagger}-\langle\hat{b}_{k}^{\ }+\hat{b}_{k}^{\dagger}\rangle\right)dW_{\mathcal{I}_{k}}(t)

(68)

For convenience, we will then write a general stochastic master equation in the form:

\displaystyle d\hat{\rho}^{c}=\mathcal{L}\hat{\rho}^{c}\penalty\ dt+\mathcal{S}_{\rm hom}[\sqrt{\gamma_{k}}\hat{b}_{k}]\hat{\rho}^{c}

(69)

where
\mathcal{L}
defines all contributions to the master equation governing deterministic evolution. We can thus write for the differential of conditional expectation of an arbitrary operator
\hat{o}
:

\displaystyle d\langle\hat{o}\rangle^{c}={\rm tr}\{\mathcal{L}\hat{\rho}^{c}\hat{o}\}\penalty\ dt+\sqrt{\frac{\gamma_{k}}{2}}\left(C^{c}_{ob_{k}}+C^{c}_{b_{k}^{\dagger}o}\right)dW_{\mathcal{I}_{k}}(t)

(70)

Analogously, we can write for arbitrary second-order moments:

\displaystyle d\langle\hat{o}_{1}\hat{o}_{2}\rangle^{c}={\rm tr}\{\mathcal{L}\hat{\rho}^{c}\hat{o}_{1}\hat{o}_{2}\}\penalty\ dt+\sqrt{\frac{\gamma_{k}}{2}}\left(\!\langle\hat{o}_{1}\hat{o}_{2}\hat{b}_{k}\rangle^{c}\!+\!\langle\hat{b}_{k}^{\dagger}\hat{o}_{1}\hat{o}_{2}\rangle^{c}\!-\!\langle\hat{o}_{1}\hat{o}_{2}\rangle^{c}\langle\hat{b}_{k}\rangle^{c}\!-\!\langle\hat{b}_{k}^{\dagger}\rangle^{c}\langle\hat{o}_{1}\hat{o}_{2}\rangle^{c}\!\right)\!dW_{\mathcal{I}_{k}}(t)

(71)

We will now use the above results obtain the equation of motion for an arbitrary
normal-ordered
cumulant
C^{c}_{o_{1}o_{2}}=\langle\hat{o}_{1}\hat{o}_{2}\rangle^{c}-\langle\hat{o}_{1}\rangle^{c}\langle\hat{o}_{2}\rangle^{c}
. The important step arises in writing the differential of this cumulant using Ito’s lemma:

\displaystyle dC^{c}_{o_{1}o_{2}}

\displaystyle=d\langle\hat{o}_{1}\hat{o}_{2}\rangle^{c}-(d\langle\hat{o}_{1}\rangle^{c})\langle\hat{o}_{2}\rangle^{c}-\langle\hat{o}_{1}\rangle^{c}(d\langle\hat{o}_{2}\rangle^{c})

\displaystyle-(d\langle\hat{o}_{1}\rangle^{c})(d\langle\hat{o}_{2}\rangle^{c})

(72)

where the term on the second line arises from the modified chain rule in Ito calculus. We can now proceed to obtaining the individual contributions to the above equation.

We start with the first term in Eq. (
72
), which was calculated in Eq. (
71
). To proceed, it will prove convenient to write moments higher than first-order in terms of their corresponding cumulants. We begin with the second-order moments:

\displaystyle d\langle\hat{o}_{1}\hat{o}_{2}\rangle^{c}={\rm tr}\{\mathcal{L}\hat{\rho}^{c}\hat{o}_{1}\hat{o}_{2}\}\penalty\ dt+

\displaystyle\sqrt{\frac{\gamma_{k}}{2}}\Big(\langle\hat{o}_{1}\hat{o}_{2}\hat{b}_{k}\rangle^{c}+\langle\hat{b}_{k}^{\dagger}\hat{o}_{1}\hat{o}_{2}\rangle^{c}-C^{c}_{{o}_{1}{o}_{2}}\langle\hat{b}_{k}\rangle^{c}-\langle\hat{b}_{k}^{\dagger}\rangle^{c}C^{c}_{{o}_{1}{o}_{2}}

\displaystyle-\langle\hat{o}_{2}\rangle^{c}\langle\hat{o}_{1}\rangle^{c}\langle\hat{b}_{k}\rangle^{c}-\langle\hat{o}_{2}\rangle^{c}\langle\hat{o}_{1}\rangle^{c}\langle\hat{b}_{k}^{\dagger}\rangle^{c}\Big)dW_{\mathcal{I}_{k}}(t)

(73)

Next, we write down the expressions relating third-order moments to their corresponding cumulants using Eq. (
58
):

\displaystyle C^{c}_{o_{1}o_{2}b_{k}}

\displaystyle=\langle\hat{o}_{1}\hat{o}_{2}\hat{b}_{k}\rangle^{c}-C^{c}_{{o}_{1}{o}_{2}}\langle\hat{b}_{k}\rangle^{c}-C^{c}_{{o}_{1}{b}_{k}}\langle\hat{o}_{2}\rangle^{c}-C^{c}_{{o}_{2}{b}_{k}}\langle\hat{o}_{1}\rangle^{c}

\displaystyle-\langle\hat{o}_{2}\rangle^{c}\langle\hat{o}_{1}\rangle^{c}\langle\hat{b}_{k}\rangle^{c}

(74a)

\displaystyle C^{c}_{b_{k}^{\dagger}o_{1}o_{2}}

\displaystyle=\langle\hat{b}_{k}^{\dagger}\hat{o}_{1}\hat{o}_{2}\rangle^{c}-C^{c}_{{o}_{1}{o}_{2}}\langle\hat{b}^{\dagger}_{k}\rangle^{c}-C^{c}_{{b}_{k}^{\dagger}{o}_{1}}\langle\hat{o}_{2}\rangle^{c}-C^{c}_{{b}_{k}^{\dagger}{o}_{2}}\langle\hat{o}_{1}\rangle^{c}

\displaystyle-\langle\hat{o}_{2}\rangle^{c}\langle\hat{o}_{1}\rangle^{c}\langle\hat{b}^{\dagger}_{k}\rangle^{c}

(74b)

employing which, Eq. (
72
) becomes:

\displaystyle d\langle\hat{o}_{1}\hat{o}_{2}\rangle^{c}={\rm tr}\{\mathcal{L}\hat{\rho}^{c}\hat{o}_{1}\hat{o}_{2}\}\penalty\ dt+

\displaystyle\sqrt{\frac{\gamma_{k}}{2}}\Big(C^{c}_{o_{1}o_{2}b_{k}}+C^{c}_{{o}_{1}{b}_{k}}\langle\hat{o}_{2}\rangle^{c}+C^{c}_{{o}_{2}{b}_{k}}\langle\hat{o}_{1}\rangle^{c}\Big)dW_{\mathcal{I}_{k}}(t)

\displaystyle+

\displaystyle\sqrt{\frac{\gamma_{k}}{2}}\Big(C^{c}_{b_{k}^{\dagger}o_{1}o_{2}}+C^{c}_{{b}_{k}^{\dagger}{o}_{1}}\langle\hat{o}_{2}\rangle^{c}+C^{c}_{{b}_{k}^{\dagger}{o}_{2}}\langle\hat{o}_{1}\rangle^{c}\Big)dW_{\mathcal{I}_{k}}(t)

(75)

where terms involving only first-order moments cancel. Note that the above includes terms of up to
O(dt)
, as
dW_{\mathcal{I}_{k}}(t)
is formally
O(dt^{1/2})
.

Next, we can calculate the second and third terms in Eq. (
72
), using Eq. (
70
). These take the simple forms:

\displaystyle(d\langle\hat{o}_{1}\rangle^{c})\langle\hat{o}_{2}\rangle^{c}

\displaystyle=\langle\hat{o}_{2}\rangle^{c}{\rm tr}\{\mathcal{L}\hat{\rho}^{c}\hat{o}_{1}\}\penalty\ dt+\sqrt{\frac{\gamma_{k}}{2}}\left(C^{c}_{o_{1}b_{k}}\langle\hat{o}_{2}\rangle^{c}+C^{c}_{b_{k}^{\dagger}o_{1}}\langle\hat{o}_{2}\rangle^{c}\right)dW_{\mathcal{I}_{k}}(t)

(76a)

\displaystyle(d\langle\hat{o}_{2}\rangle^{c})\langle\hat{o}_{1}\rangle^{c}

\displaystyle=\langle\hat{o}_{1}\rangle^{c}{\rm tr}\{\mathcal{L}\hat{\rho}^{c}\hat{o}_{2}\}\penalty\ dt+\sqrt{\frac{\gamma_{k}}{2}}\left(C^{c}_{o_{2}b_{k}}\langle\hat{o}_{1}\rangle^{c}+C^{c}_{b_{k}^{\dagger}o_{2}}\langle\hat{o}_{1}\rangle^{c}\right)dW_{\mathcal{I}_{k}}(t)

(76b)

Both equations above once again contain terms up to
O(dt)
.

Finally, we can write down the term arising from Ito’s lemma. The requiring differential is that given by Eq. (
70
). However, we need only retain the lowest
O(dt)
term here, which is given by:

\displaystyle(d\langle\hat{o}_{1}\rangle^{c})(d\langle\hat{o}_{2}\rangle^{c})=\frac{\gamma_{k}}{2}\left(C^{c}_{o_{1}b_{k}}+C^{c}_{b_{k}^{\dagger}o_{1}}\right)\left(C^{c}_{o_{2}b_{k}}+C^{c}_{b_{k}^{\dagger}o_{2}}\right)\penalty\ dt+O(dt^{3/2})

(77)

where we have used
(dW_{\mathcal{I}_{k}})^{2}=dt
.

Finally, we can write down Eq. (
72
) by combining Eqs. (
75
), (
76a
), (
76b
), and (
77
). This finally yields:

\displaystyle dC^{c}_{o_{1}o_{2}}={\rm tr}\{\mathcal{L}\hat{\rho}^{c}\hat{o}_{1}\hat{o}_{2}\!-\!\langle\hat{o}_{2}\rangle^{c}\mathcal{L}\hat{\rho}^{c}\hat{o}_{1}\!-\!\langle\hat{o}_{1}\rangle^{c}\mathcal{L}\hat{\rho}^{c}\hat{o}_{2}\}dt

\displaystyle\penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ -\frac{\gamma_{k}}{2}\left(C^{c}_{o_{1}b_{k}}+C^{c}_{b_{k}^{\dagger}o_{1}}\right)\left(C^{c}_{o_{2}b_{k}}+C^{c}_{b_{k}^{\dagger}o_{2}}\right)dt

\displaystyle\penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ +\sqrt{\frac{\gamma_{k}}{2}}\left(C^{c}_{o_{1}o_{2}b_{k}}+C^{c}_{b_{k}^{\dagger}o_{1}o_{2}}\right)dW_{\mathcal{I}_{k}}(t)

(78)

The first term on the right hand side describes deterministic dynamics governed by
\mathcal{L}
. The second term is due to the measurement, but does not depend on the stochastic Wiener increment
dW_{\mathcal{I}_{k}}(t)
. The last term is a stochastic contribution; however note that it is related only to third-order cumulants. Within the ansätz used in this paper, this explicitly stochastic term vanishes.

C.3
Numerical tests: unconditional dynamics and comparison to exact results

We will use the single coherently-driven QNP mode modeled as a Kerr oscillator to benchmark the TEOMs approach. Using the results of Sec.
C.1
of the SI, and following truncation with
n_{\rm trunc}=2
, the single Kerr oscillator is defined by the first order cumulant
C_{b}\equiv\langle\hat{b}\rangle
governed by the equation,

\displaystyle\langle\dot{\hat{b}}\rangle=\left(i\Delta_{1}-\frac{{\gamma}}{2}\right)\langle\hat{b}\rangle+i\Lambda_{1}\langle\hat{b}^{\dagger}\rangle\langle\hat{b}\rangle\langle\hat{b}\rangle-i\eta_{b}+i\Lambda_{1}\left(C_{bb}\langle\hat{b}^{\dagger}\rangle+2C_{b^{\dagger}b}\langle\hat{b}\rangle\right)

(79)

as well as the second-order cumulants
C_{bb}=\langle\hat{b}\hat{b}\rangle-\langle\hat{b}\rangle^{2}
,
C_{b^{\dagger}b}=\langle\hat{b}^{\dagger}\hat{b}\rangle-\langle\hat{b}^{\dagger}\rangle\langle\hat{b}\rangle
, defined by

\displaystyle\dot{C}_{b^{\dagger}b}

\displaystyle=-{\gamma}C_{b^{\dagger}b}-i\Lambda_{1}(C_{bb}\langle\hat{b}^{\dagger}\rangle^{2}-C_{bb}^{*}\langle\hat{b}\rangle^{2}),

(80a)

\displaystyle\dot{C}_{bb}

\displaystyle=\left(i2\Delta_{1}-{\gamma}+i\Lambda_{1}(1+4|\langle\hat{b}\rangle|^{2}\!\!+6C_{b^{\dagger}b})\right)C_{bb}+i\Lambda_{1}\langle\hat{b}\rangle^{2}(1+2C_{b^{\dagger}b}).

(80b)

Eqs. (
79
)-(
80b
) together comprise the TEOMs for the single Kerr oscillator with
n_{\rm trunc}=2
.

It is reasonable to assume that such a truncation must be valid only in a certain subset of the full parameter space defining the system. To identify the general constraints on such a truncation, we make extensive use of the exact quantum steady-state solution of the coherently-driven Kerr oscillator, obtained via the complex-
\mathcal{P}
representation
Drummond and Walls 1980
; details can be found in SI Sec.
J
. This provides access to exact steady-state cumulants of arbitrary order, which help us benchmark the accuracy of the TEOM approach.

One such regime should be furnished by the
classical limit
of the single-mode Kerr QNP, where the QNP is always in a coherent state and thus described entirely by its first-order cumulants. To identify this classical limit, and thus explore deviations from it due to quantum effects, it proves convenient to work with specific scaled quantities: after introducing dimensionless time
t^{\prime}={\gamma}t
and energy scales
(\Delta_{1}^{\prime},\Lambda_{1}^{\prime},\eta_{b}^{\prime})=(\Delta_{1},\Lambda_{1},\eta_{b})/{\gamma}
, we scale
\langle\hat{b}\rangle^{\prime}\to\sqrt{\Lambda_{1}^{\prime}}\langle\hat{b}\rangle
, and impose our truncated ansatz, following which Eq. (
79
) becomes

\displaystyle\frac{d}{dt^{\prime}}\langle\hat{b}\rangle^{\prime}=\left(i\Delta_{1}^{\prime}-\frac{1}{2}\right)\langle\hat{b}\rangle^{\prime}+i\langle\hat{b}^{\dagger}\rangle^{\prime}\langle\hat{b}\rangle^{\prime}\langle\hat{b}\rangle^{\prime}-i\mathcal{E}+i\Lambda_{1}^{\prime}\left(C_{bb}\langle\hat{b}^{\dagger}\rangle^{\prime}+2C_{b^{\dagger}b}\langle\hat{b}\rangle^{\prime}\right).

(81)

The first line in Eq. (
81
) makes no reference to second-order cumulants of the QNP mode state; it thus describes dynamics when the QNP is in a coherent state (for which such cumulants vanish), corresponding to the classical limit. Dynamics in this limit depend on the drive and the nonlinearity via a single dimensionless parameter
Dykman and Krivoglaz 1979
;
Dykman 2012
,

\displaystyle\mathcal{E}=\eta^{\prime}\sqrt{\Lambda^{\prime}}=\frac{\eta_{b}}{{\gamma}}\sqrt{\frac{\Lambda_{1}}{{\gamma}}}.

(82)

The second line of Eq. (
81
) does however depend on cumulants; these terms describe quantum dynamics that lead to a deviation from purely coherent state evolution. In these scaled units, the TEOMs for the second-order cumulants obtained from Eq. (
80a
), (
80b
) take the form

\displaystyle\frac{d}{dt^{\prime}}{C}_{b^{\dagger}b}

\displaystyle=-C_{b^{\dagger}b}-i(C_{bb}(\langle\hat{b}^{\dagger}\rangle^{\prime})^{2}-C_{bb}^{*}(\langle\hat{b}\rangle^{\prime})^{2}),

(83a)

\displaystyle\frac{d}{dt^{\prime}}{C}_{bb}

\displaystyle=\left(i2\Delta_{1}^{\prime}-1+i(\Lambda_{1}^{\prime}+4|\langle\hat{b}\rangle^{\prime}|^{2}\!\!+6\Lambda_{1}^{\prime}C_{b^{\dagger}b})\right)C_{bb}+i(\langle\hat{b}\rangle^{\prime})^{2}(1+2C_{b^{\dagger}b}).

(83b)

Consider now the transformation where
\Lambda_{1}^{\prime}\to 0
, while
\mathcal{E}
is held fixed (by increasing the drive strength
\eta_{b}^{\prime}
simultaneously). Terms in the first line of Eq. (
81
) describing classical dynamics remain unchanged, while those in the second line describing quantum deviations become smaller,
provided
second-order cumulants do not grow with
\Lambda_{1}^{\prime}
. From Eqs. (
83a
), (
83b
), it is clear that
C_{b^{\dagger}b}
is independent of
\Lambda_{1}^{\prime}
, while
C_{bb}
has a dependence that is negligible as
\Lambda_{1}^{\prime}\to 0
for
|\langle\hat{b}\rangle^{\prime}|\neq 0
. Hence by keeping
\mathcal{E}
fixed while decreasing the nonlinearity strength, the influence of second-order cumulants on dynamics of the QNP mode amplitude is suppressed, describing the classical limit of QNP operation. Physically, this transformation leads to an increase in the unscaled drive
\eta^{\prime}
and QNP mode amplitude
|\langle\hat{b}\rangle|
, and hence occupation number, decreasing the relative impact of quantum fluctuations in agreement with conventional notions of classicality.
Conversely, increasing nonlinearity strength for fixed
\mathcal{E}
should enhance the impact of quantum fluctuations, marked by a systematic increase in second-order cumulants, and taking the Kerr QNP gradually towards a quantum regime of operation.

To explore this transition, we start with the classical limit of the QNP as determined by terms in the first line of Eq. (
81
), for which the steady-state phase diagram can be easily computed (see SI
SI
). This phase diagram, shown in
(\Delta_{1},\mathcal{E})
-space in the center panel of Fig.
12
(a), is the well-known result for a coherently-driven classical Kerr oscillator
Dykman and Krivoglaz 1979
;
Dykman 2012
: the orange region depicts the classical bistability, which emerges for sufficiently negative detuning given our choice of the sign of the Kerr nonlinearity.

Figure 12:
Classical and quantum dynamics of a single Kerr QNP mode. In this plot, colored solid curves are exact complex-
\mathcal{P}
results, colored dots are TEOMs simulations in the long-time limit, and dashed orange curves are classical results. Other curves are specified when relevant. (a) Center panel:
Classical
phase diagram of the single coherently-driven Kerr QNP mode in (
\Delta_{1},\mathcal{E}
) space. Left panel: QNP amplitude as a function of
\mathcal{E}
for fixed detuning
\Delta_{1}/\gamma_{1}=-1.0
(across indicated vertical cross-section of the phase diagram). Note that the total damping rate
{\gamma}=\gamma_{1}+\Gamma_{1}
, and
\Gamma_{1}=\gamma_{1}/2
. Right panel: QNP amplitude as a function of detuning
\Delta_{1}
for fixed
\mathcal{E}=0.385
(across indicated horizontal cross-section of the phase diagram). Dashed black lines in both plots indicate the response for a linear oscillator. (b) Steady-state scaled amplitude
|\langle\hat{b}^{\prime}\rangle|
for nonlinearity strengths
\Lambda_{1}/\gamma_{1}\in[0.005,0.02,0.05]
, as a function of
\mathcal{E}
for fixed
\Delta_{1}/\gamma_{1}=-1.0
(region within the gray box in (a), left panel). (c) Same as (b) but as a function of detuning
\Delta_{1}
for fixed
\mathcal{E}=0.385
(region within the gray box in (a), right panel). (d) Steady-state second-order cumulants (
C_{b^{\dagger}b}
, purple, and
|C_{bb}|
, green) and higher (third, fourth) order cumulants (dashed gray) across the cross-section in (b); nonlinearity increases from left to right. (e) Same as (d) but now for the cross-section in (c).

This bistability is a feature specific to nonlinear systems; in its vicinity, we can expect an enhanced nonlinear response of the QNP observables to QNP inputs. This is explicitly observed in the scaled field amplitude
|\langle\hat{b}\rangle^{\prime}|
, plotted in Fig.
12
(b) as a function of
\mathcal{E}
for fixed
\Delta/\gamma_{1}=-1.0
(that is, across the indicated vertical dashed line of the classical phase diagram). The solid blue curve is the exact complex-
P
result for
\Lambda/\gamma_{1}=0.005
, while blue dots are obtained by evolving the TEOMs, Eqs. (
79
), (
80a
), (
80b
), to their steady state numerically. For small
\mathcal{E}
, the response follows that of a linear oscillator (dashed black line), but becomes nonlinear with increasing
\mathcal{E}
. Also shown in dashed orange is the response of the classical nonlinear oscillator (explicitly dropping second-order cumulants), which does not depend on the actual nonlinearity strength but only on
\mathcal{E}
.

The influence of nonlinearity is similarly evident when plotting
|\langle\hat{b}^{\prime}\rangle|
as a function of
\Delta
, for fixed
\mathcal{E}=0.385
in Fig.
12
(c) (across the horizontal dashed line of the classical phase diagram). The black dashed curve indicates the standard Lorentzian response of a linear oscillator, centered around the bare frequency
\omega_{b}
. The response of the nonlinear QNP mode is clearly that of a Lorentzian deformed towards lower frequencies due to the Kerr-induced frequency shift.

In both results, we find excellent agreement between the exact quantum results, the TEOMs,
and
classical nonlinear dynamics. This is indicative of operating parameters where not only is a truncated description using first and second-order cumulants sufficient, but the influence of second-order cumulants on first-order cumulants is negligible as well. Consequently, we expect the QNP mode here to function as a classical reservoir mode, still allowing for nonlinear processing of information encoded in the drive amplitude, phase, and/or frequency.

If we now keep
\mathcal{E}
fixed but increase the nonlinearity strength
\Lambda
, the QNP can be moved to a quantum regime of operation. We plot the QNP response as a function of
\mathcal{E}
for
\Delta/\gamma_{1}=-1.0
in Fig.
12
(b), and as a function of
\Delta
for
\mathcal{E}=0.385
in Fig.
12
(c), for three different nonlinearity strengths
\Lambda/\gamma_{1}\in[0.005,0.02,0.05]
. In both cases, we see that with increasing nonlinearity, the response deviates from that of the classical nonlinear oscillator (which recall is essentially equivalent to the quantum result for weak
\Lambda/\gamma_{1}=0.005
). From Eq. (
81
), it is clear that this deviation is due to the coupling of first-order moments to second-order cumulants; this is precisely the effect accounted for by the TEOMs, which thus agree with the full quantum result.

Also shown in Figs.
12
(d) and (e) are steady-state cumulants plotted across the same cross-sections as Figs.
12
(b) and (c) respectively, with panels from left to right indicating stronger nonlinearities. Second-order cumulants are plotted in green and purple (lines are exact complex-
\mathcal{P}
results, dots are TEOMs); they clearly display non-monotonic behaviour as a function of
\mathcal{E}
and
\Delta
, acquiring large magnitudes for specific operating parameters in the vicinity of the classical bistability. Physically, second-order cumulants are related to the maximum and minimum (dimensionless) quadrature variances of the QNP modes:
\frac{1}{2}+C_{b^{\dagger}b}\pm|C_{bb}|
respectively, and their magnitudes are thus measures of amplification and squeezing of quantum fluctuations due to the underlying Kerr nonlinearity.

Third and fourth order cumulants are shown in the same plots in dashed gray (exact complex-
\mathcal{P}
only). There are two features of note. Firstly, the overall magnitude of these higher-order cumulants relative to second-order cumulants appears to increase for stronger nonlinearities. This is an indicator of the emergence of non-Gaussian features with increasing
\Lambda
, as expected, and generally leads to greater deviation between TEOMs and exact quantum results for first and second-order cumulants. Secondly, even for a fixed nonlinearity strength, the magnitude of cumulants depends on operating parameters (
\mathcal{E}
and
\Delta
) that lead to operation in the vicinity of the classical bistability. Hence, while results obtained from TEOMs for such operating parameters can deviate from the exact quantum solution, away from these regions the agreement improves, even for strong nonlinearities.

C.4
Numerical tests: conditional dynamics

We have so far used steady-state, unconditional quantities to analyze the nonlinear processing capabilities of a single Kerr-based QNP mode, and identified parameter regimes where an approach based on truncated EOMs is valid. However, to allow for processing of time-dependent information by the QNP, and to analyze the output from the QNP obtained as
individual
measurement records, an approach is needed that is able to accurately capture the
conditional, dynamical
evolution of the QNP mode. This is described by the SME

\displaystyle d\hat{\rho}^{c}=\mathcal{L}\hat{\rho}^{c}\penalty\ dt+\mathcal{S}[\sqrt{\gamma_{1}}\hat{b}]\hat{\rho}^{c}

(84)

where
\mathcal{S}[\sqrt{\gamma_{1}}\hat{b}]
is defined as,

\displaystyle\mathcal{S}[\sqrt{\gamma_{1}}\hat{b}_{k}]\hat{\rho}^{c}=\sqrt{\frac{\gamma_{1}}{2}}\!\left(\hat{b}_{k}\hat{\rho}^{c}+\hat{\rho}^{c}\hat{b}_{k}^{\dagger}-\langle\hat{b}_{k}+\hat{b}_{k}^{\dagger}\rangle^{c}\right)\!dW_{\mathcal{I}_{k}}\!(t)\penalty\

\displaystyle\penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ +\sqrt{\frac{\gamma_{1}}{2}}\!\left(-i\hat{b}_{k}\hat{\rho}^{c}+i\hat{\rho}^{c}\hat{b}_{k}^{\dagger}-\langle-i\hat{b}_{k}+i\hat{b}_{k}^{\dagger}\rangle^{c}\right)\!dW_{\mathcal{Q}_{k}}\!(t)

(85)

and our current system assumes the
K=1
case. Note the slight differences to Eq. (
23
) of the main text, which are only for purposes of exposition: the dissipator in Eq. (
23
) is absorbed into
\mathcal{L}
here, to aid the discussion in the previous subsection.

Expectation values with respect to the conditional quantum state under measurement
\hat{\rho}^{c}
are now conditional, which we indicate by the superscript
c
. For example, the
variation

d\langle\hat{b}\rangle^{c}

in a time
dt
is now given by
{\rm tr}\{(\mathcal{L}\penalty\ dt+\mathcal{S}[\hat{b})]\hat{\rho}^{c}\hat{b}\}
, which takes the form (as derived in Sec.
C.2
of the SI)

\displaystyle d\langle\hat{b}\rangle^{c}={\rm tr}\{(\mathcal{L}\hat{\rho}^{c}\hat{b}\}\penalty\ dt+

\displaystyle\sqrt{\frac{\gamma_{1}}{2}}\left(C^{c}_{b^{\dagger}b}+C^{c}_{bb}\right)dW^{X}(t)+i\sqrt{\frac{\gamma_{1}}{2}}\left(C^{c}_{b^{\dagger}b}-C^{c}_{bb}\right)dW^{P}(t).

(86)

The first line
\propto\mathcal{L}
simply includes terms from Eq. (
79
), with expectation values replaced by their conditional counterparts. Terms in the second line are due to the measurement superoperator, and render the equation of motion for
\langle\hat{b}\rangle^{c}

stochastic
, conditioned on the trajectory-specific realizations of
dW^{X,P}(t)
. Dropping cumulants higher than second order in Eq. (
86
), arising in the first line, yields the
stochastic
truncated EOM (STEOM) for
\langle\hat{b}\rangle^{c}
.

We can similarly obtain truncated equations of motion for the conditional cumulants
C^{c}_{bb}
,
C^{c}_{b^{\dagger}b}
under heterodyne measurement (as derived in Sec.
C.2
of the SI):

\displaystyle dC^{c}_{b^{\dagger}b}

\displaystyle=\left[{\rm tr}\{\mathcal{L}\hat{\rho}^{c}\hat{b}^{\dagger}\hat{b}\}\!-\!\langle\hat{b}^{\dagger}\rangle^{c}{\rm tr}\{\mathcal{L}\hat{\rho}^{c}\hat{b}\}\!-\!\langle\hat{b}\rangle^{c}{\rm tr}\{\mathcal{L}\hat{\rho}^{c}\hat{b}^{\dagger}\}\right]\!dt

\displaystyle\penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ -{\gamma_{1}}\left[(C^{c}_{b^{\dagger}b})^{2}+C^{c}_{bb}(C^{c}_{bb})^{*}\right]\penalty\ dt,

(87a)

\displaystyle dC^{c}_{bb}

\displaystyle=\left[{\rm tr}\{\mathcal{L}\hat{\rho}^{c}\hat{b}\hat{b}\}-2\langle\hat{b}\rangle^{c}{\rm tr}\{\mathcal{L}\hat{\rho}^{c}\hat{b}\}\right]dt

\displaystyle\penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ -2{\gamma_{1}}C^{c}_{bb}C^{c}_{b^{\dagger}b}\penalty\ dt.

(87b)

Again, terms
\propto\mathcal{L}
are as found in Eqs. (
80a
), (
80b
) post-truncation, with expectation values replaced by their conditional counterparts. The second line of each equation describes the evolution due to measurement, which at first glance appears deterministic: note that Wiener increments
dW^{X,P}(t)
make no appearance. For
linear
quantum systems under continuous weak measurement, this is in fact the case: second-order cumulants form a closed set described by the above equations, and no stochastic terms arise
Breslin and Milburn 1997
;
Doherty and Jacobs 1999
;
Černotík et al. 2015
;
Zhang and Mølmer 2017
. However, for
nonlinear
quantum systems of interest here, second-order cumulants can couple to the
stochastic
first-order moments (here, via terms
\propto\mathcal{L}
), rendering the conditional evolution of second-order cumulants generally stochastic as well.

Figure 13:
Benchmarking STEOMs against full SME simulations. Simulated parameters are as marked by the teal ‘X’ in the phase diagram of Fig.
12
(a):
\mathcal{E}=0.385
,
\Delta_{1}/\gamma_{1}=-1.0
, for (a)
\Lambda_{1}/\gamma_{1}=0.005
and (b)
\Lambda_{1}/\gamma_{1}=0.02
. Note that the total damping rate
{\gamma}=\gamma_{1}+\Gamma_{1}
, and
\Gamma_{1}=\gamma_{1}/2
. Left panel shows real and imaginary parts of the conditional expectation value
\langle\hat{b}\rangle
, and center panel shows conditional second-order cumulants
C_{bb},C_{b^{\dagger}b}
; colored (white) curves are SME (STEOMs) simulations. Right panel shows measured quadratures
I(t)
,
Q(t)
: the SME result is plotted in solid black, while the STEOMs result is plotted in yellow, with a lighter shade indicating earlier times. (c) Measured quadratures
\{I(t),Q(t)\}
at
\gamma t=20.0
obtained using SME (left panel) and STEOMs (right panel), for
\mathcal{E}=0.385
,
\Lambda_{1}/\gamma_{1}=0.02
, and
\Delta_{1}/\gamma_{1}=-1.0
. (d) Same as (c) but for
\Delta_{1}/\gamma_{1}=0.0
.

Eqs. (
86
), (
87a
), (
87b
) define the STEOMs for the single Kerr QNP mode
under continuous heterodyne monitoring
. To assess their validity, we compare their simulation results against integration of the SME, Eq. (
84
). For concreteness, we consider the operating point marked by the teal ‘X’ in the phase diagram of Fig.
12
(a):
\mathcal{E}=0.385
,
\Delta/\gamma=-1.0
. Note that the
same noise realizations

dW^{X,P}(t)
must always be used for both simulations to ensure the compared trajectories are conditioned on the same measurement record. Results are included in Fig.
13
(a), (b) for
\Lambda/\gamma\in[0.005,0.02]
respectively, showing first-order moments (left panel) and second-order cumulants (center panel), with STEOMs results in white, and SME results obtained using QuTiP
Johansson et al. 2013
in color. Both methods agree very well, especially for first-order cumulants. In particular, note the stochastic nature of second-order cumulants, which is a direct signature of the nonlinearity of the quantum system under study, and is further enhanced at strong nonlinearities. However, for the same temporal resolution, SME simulations with a Hilbert space cutoff of 625 photons (around 3 times larger than the maximum occupation number, a typical conservative value) demand several orders of magnitude more simulation time than the STEOMs.

However, in a real experiment, such information about the QNP state must be extracted from measurement records. For the case of heterodyne measurement of the single Kerr oscillator, the obtained records
\mathcal{I}(t)\equiv\mathcal{I}_{1}(t),\mathcal{Q}(t)\equiv\mathcal{Q}_{1}(t)
are defined in Eqs. (
4
) of the main text (here we take
\bar{n}_{\rm cl}=0
, ignoring the role of classical readout noise). These records are typically processed to reduce noise, most commonly via temporal filtering; the processed records define
measured quadratures
as in the main text,

\displaystyle I(t)=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2\mathcal{T}}}\int_{t-\mathcal{T}}^{t}d\tau\penalty\ \mathcal{I}(\tau);\penalty\ Q(t)=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2\mathcal{T}}}\int_{t-\mathcal{T}}^{t}d\tau\penalty\ \mathcal{Q}(\tau).

(88)

which here describe processing of single-shot readout records. In this section, we take
t-\mathcal{T}=0
for filtering over the entire collection window. Measured quadratures obtained using a single set of measurement records are shown in the measured phase space given by
(I(t),Q(t))
in the right panel of Fig.
13
(a), (b)
\Lambda/\gamma\in[0.005,0.02]
as before; SME results are shown in solid black, with the STEOMs results plotted on top, using a colorscale going from black to yellow indicating increasing time. Both show excellent agreement; this is unsurprising since
{I}(t),{Q}(t)
yield information about the underlying conditional dynamics of
\langle\hat{b}\rangle^{c}
, which also agree very well between the two methods.

Note that the measured quadratures are themselves stochastic quantities, whose statistics are correlated with the underlying measured system state. This can be seen via the measured quadrature distributions in Fig.
13
(c), obtained by simulating several measurement records (here
100
in total) and plotting
\{I(t),Q(t)\}
at
\gamma t=20.0
, for
\Delta_{1}/\gamma_{1}=-1.0
as before. At this operating point near the classical bistable region, the Kerr nonlinearity amplifies the magnitude of cumulants
C_{bb}\equiv|C_{bb}|e^{i\phi_{bb}}
[see Fig.
12
(d)] leading to squeezing of the internal QNP field along the axis determined by
\phi_{bb}/2
(and amplification along the orthogonal quadrature). This internal QNP squeezing manifests as squeezing of the measured quadrature distribution; the squeezing axis is unchanged since the temporal filter defining measured quadratures via Eq. (
88
) is quadrature-agnostic, and thus only reduces the overall noise power while preserving its relative strength amongst measured quadratures. On the other hand, for
\Delta_{1}/\gamma_{1}=0.0
, further away from the bistable region,
C_{bb}
is much smaller in magnitude and the measured quadrature distribution in Fig.
13
(d) exhibits no squeezing. Excellent agreement between SME (left) and STEOMs (right) is observed for both cases.

C.5
Non-Gaussian signatures captured by STEOM simulations

The (S)TEOMs approach to nonlinear quantum systems in this work uses
n_{\rm trunc}=2
, so that only cumulants of up to second-order are retained. Gaussian states have at most second-order cumulants as well. However, the TEOMs approach is distinct from other methods of analyzing nonlinear quantum systems where fluctuations are treated within a Gaussian approximation, such as standard linearization. There are two important indicators of this difference. First is the deviation of first-order cumulants from their classical values, observed in Fig.
12
, which would not happen in standard linearization schemes. The second indicator appears in stochastic dynamics of second-order cumulants, as seen in Fig.
13
, which would be perfectly deterministic for a quantum system whose state is defined
exactly
by a Gaussian description.

These features indicate the ability of (S)TEOMs to simulate dynamics beyond those of just Gaussian states. In fact, we find that the STEOMs can exhibit non-Gaussian characteristics in measured heterodyne outputs that are also captured by SME simulations. An example is depicted in Fig.
14
, where we show measured distributions obtained using the SME and STEOMs for a Kerr oscillator operated in the vicinity of the classical Kerr bistability (for parameters, see caption). Both measured distributions clearly display ‘crescent’-shaped deformations typical of non-Gaussian characteristics observed in transient dynamics of Kerr-type nonlinear oscillators
Boutin et al. 2017
.

Figure 14:
Non-Gaussian signatures in single Kerr oscillator heterodyne measurements. Measured quadratures
\{I(t),Q(t)\}
at
\gamma_{1}t=60.0
obtained using SME (left panel) and STEOMs (right panel), for
\mathcal{E}=0.369
,
\Lambda_{1}/\gamma_{1}=0.0295
, and
\Delta_{1}/\gamma_{1}=-1.0
. Note that the total damping rate
{\gamma}=\gamma_{1}+\Gamma_{1}
, and
\Gamma_{1}=\gamma_{1}/2
.

C.6
Validity and scalability of (S)TEOMs

The key observations from our analysis of Kerr QNP operating regimes and the generation of higher-order cumulants (summarized in Fig.
12
) are twofold. Primarily, the magnitude of nonzero higher-order cumulants increases with the strength of the Kerr nonlinearity relative to the QNP mode damping rate,
\Lambda/{\gamma}
(where
\gamma
is the total QNP mode loss rate), which coincides with the QNP under coherent driving transitioning from classical to quantum regimes. However, operating regimes in the vicinity of the classical bistability also lead to an enhancement in higher-order cumulants, regardless of nonlinearity strength. To be confident of the validity of the truncated cumulants approach, we therefore analyze QNPs with nonlinearity strengths
\Lambda/{\gamma}\leq 0.02
, and operate
near
but not within the classically-bistable region.

This truncated cumulants approach provides a highly efficient mathematical description when applied to multimode quantum systems. For a measurement chain comprising an
M
-mode quantum system coupled to a
K
-mode QNP, the composite system of
R=M+K
total quantum modes is described by
2R^{2}+3R
unknowns using (S)TEOMs, scaling quadratically with
R
instead of the exponential growth in required Hilbert space size for full (S)ME simulations. This description also places no constraints on modal occupation numbers, enabling our exploration of a well-defined classical limit of the coherently-driven Kerr QNP. Our (S)TEOMs approach is built to be a scalable theoretical framework via an efficient computer-algebra implementation, which allows the calculation of equations of motion of retained cumulants up to order
n_{\rm trunc}
(here, second-order) for nonlinear quantum systems comprising arbitrary numbers of bosonic modes under continuous measurement. Supplementary benchmarking simulations for systems with more than one mode are included in Sec.
I
of the SI. In the main text, we employ (S)TEOMs to simulate dynamics of measurement chains comprising measured quantum systems coupled to multimode QNPs, while operating within the aforementioned constraints.

Appendix D
Quantum properties of the quantum system (QS) states to be distinguished

In this section we analyze the quantum system (QS) defined by Eq. (
12
) of the main text, whose states we wish to distinguish in classification tasks. The TEOMs that describe the dynamics of the QS are simply given by the QS sector of Eqs. (
7a
), (
8
), and are
exact
for
n_{\rm trunc}=2
, owing to the QS linearity under coherent driving, and its non-reciprocal coupling to the QNP. For convenience, we reproduce the TEOMs below,
